Figure 9a–d illustrates the impact of the Schmidt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sc)}_{thnf}$$\end{document} on the velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}^{\prime}(\xi )$$\end{document}, temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, Concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\xi )$$\end{document}, and microbial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document} gradients, respectively. The Schmidt number is a dimensionless quantity defined as the ratio of liquid viscosity to mass diffusivity. Therefore, if the viscosity of the liquid exceeds the mass diffusivity, a higher Schmidt number will be present throughout the entire boundary layer. As the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sc)}_{thnf}$$\end{document} rises, the concentration diminishes. This reduction leads to a decrease in the buoyancy effects of concentration, resulting in a lower liquid velocity. The declines in velocity, temperature, and concentration gradients are accompanied by a simultaneous thickening of the momentum and concentration boundary layers, which is similar to the effect of an increasing Prandtl number on the thickness of a thermal boundary layer. Figure 9d illustrates that with an increase in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sc)}_{thnf}$$\end{document}, there is a corresponding decrease in the microbial gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document}. This indicates that the microorganisms are diffusing at a slower rate through the liquid. In the flow's nodal region, the diminished mass diffusivity restricts the dispersion of microorganisms, resulting in a more concentrated distribution and a reduction in the overall microbial gradient. Figure 10a, b signifies the impact of bio-convection Lewis number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Lb)}_{thnf}$$\end{document} and micro-organism difference factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(\delta }_{1})$$\end{document} via microbial gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document} respectively. It can be clearly noticed that the increasing values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Lb)}_{thnf}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(\delta }_{1})$$\end{document} diminished the microbial gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi ).$$\end{document}

Figure 11a–d illustrates how the velocity slip factor λ affect the velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}^{\prime}\left(\xi \right),$$\end{document} temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, Concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\xi )$$\end{document}, and microbial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document} gradients, respectively. It is observed that an increase in the velocity slip factor λ leads to a higher velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}^{\prime}(\xi )$$\end{document} gradient but decline the thermal boundary layer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, Concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\xi )$$\end{document}, and microbial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document} gradients. By the stronger slip λ at the boundary, the liquid near the wall doesn’t stick as tightly, so it moves more freely. This raises the overall velocity gradient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${f}^{\prime}\left(\xi \right)$$\end{document} because less momentum is wasted overcoming friction at the wall. Meanwhile, the layers through which heat, solute, or microbes diffuse toward the wall become thinner, the faster moving liquid carries away energy, species, or microbes more quickly, giving them less time to accumulate near the boundary. As a result, the temperature \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, concentration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi (\xi )$$\end{document}, and microbial density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (\xi )$$\end{document} near the surface drop compared to the no-slip case.

Fig. 11Influence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \;\lambda \; on \;$$\end{document} a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; {f}^{\prime}(\xi )$$\end{document}, b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, c \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \Phi (\xi )$$\end{document} and d \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \chi (\xi )$$\end{document}

Influence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \;\lambda \; on \;$$\end{document} a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; {f}^{\prime}(\xi )$$\end{document}, b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \theta (\xi )$$\end{document}, c \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \Phi (\xi )$$\end{document} and d \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; \chi (\xi )$$\end{document}

Optimizing heat, solute and microbes transfer rates using RSM via CCD model

For the specified numerical values of the factors influencing flow phenomena, the rate coefficients, such as the heat transfer rate represented by the Nusselt number, the mass transfer rate indicated by the Sherwood number, and the microorganism transfer rate denoted by the motile density factor, are calculated. To achieve an optimized heat and mass transfer rate for the predicted values, a statistical method known as RSM with CCD is beneficial for simulating variations in different factors. Factors like the volume fraction of nanoparticles (A, B, C) and the unsteady factor (S) are utilized to determine the responses of the Nusselt number, Sherwood number, and motile density factor, and these are then briefly explained. The current research provides an illustration of how the incorporated the independent factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A, B, C, \mathrm{and}\text{ } S)$$\end{document} influence the response function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N{u}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{h}_{x}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{h}_{n}$$\end{document} when using the statistical RSM methodology. The simulation is presented for their specific range, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.01\le A\le 0.05$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.01\le B\le 0.05$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.01\le C\le 0.05$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.1\le S\le 0.6$$\end{document}, and these values are deployed in Table 6. The simulation process is conducted using a quadratic order model, which incorporates an in-built CCD utilizing a face-centered technique that encompasses various levels, including low, middle, and high values. Therefore, Table 7 exhibits the computed values of responses of the Nusselt number, Sherwood number and motile density factor results for the factors A, B, C, and S. The allocations of the responses are presented for 30 usual runs. These standard CCD experimental runs represent center point replicates where all factors are maintained at mid-levels (A=0.03, B=0.03, C=0.03, S=0.35). These replicates are essential for estimating pure experimental error, assessing numerical reproducibility, and validating model adequacy in RSM analysis. Table 8 depicts the standard deviation for the quadratic model with a mean and the actual values of the R adjusted and predicted values are also displayed. In accordance with the stated, the structure of the quadratic polynomial model is established using Minitab Statistical software as follows:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Response = \alpha_{0} + \mathop \sum \limits_{i = 1}^{4} \alpha_{i} x_{i} + \mathop \sum \limits_{i = 1}^{4} \alpha_{ii} x_{i}^{2} + \mathop \sum \limits_{i = 1}^{4} \mathop \sum \limits_{j = 1}^{4} \alpha_{ij} x_{i} x_{j} , $$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\alpha}_{0}$$\end{document} is intercept term, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\alpha}_{i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\alpha}_{ii}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\alpha}_{ij}$$\end{document} are regression coefficients of the linear, quadratic and interaction factors respectively.

ANOVA analysis

Accuracy of the model

The model's accuracy is utilized for testing, thus an effective statistical method, namely "ANalysis Of Variance" (ANOVA), is recommended, with results illustrated in Table 8. The model F-value indicates that the proposed model holds significance. However, it is important to note that there is a 0.01 probability that a high F-value may arise from noise. The simulated P-values demonstrate numeric values below 0.0500, signifying that the model terms are indeed significant. Therefore, it is suggested that the model terms are not significant if the values exceed 0.1000. If multiple model terms are deemed insignificant, the proposed model may represent the design space. The ANOVA results presented in Table 8 demonstrate exceptional model validity for all three responses. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, the model F-value of 64,027.04 with p < 0.000 indicates the quadratic model is highly significant, with linear terms contributing most strongly (F = 223,062.03), followed by interaction effects (F = 504.98) and square terms (F = 275.12). Similarly, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}, the model F-value of 6577.34 confirms significance, with linear effects dominating (F = 22,511.72), while square and interaction terms (F = 147.54 and 240.96) capture important curvature and interactive effects. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}, the F-value of 2,581.25 validates the model, with linear terms again being most influential (F = 8,798.68). The negligible lack-of-fit and pure error terms confirm model adequacy, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values exceeding 99.9% for all responses indicate that the quadratic models account for virtually all variability in the data. The high adjusted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} and predicted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^2$$\end{document} values (differing by < 0.2%) further confirm excellent model fit without overfitting, validating the use of Eqs. (14–16) for predictive optimization in practical applications.

Table 9 shows the estimated regression coefficients for the responses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({Nu}_{x},{Sh}_{x} and {Sh}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} with its corresponding P-value set for the non-linear polynomial model in Eq. (28). It is worthy of attention that a large P-value indicates that the change in input has no noticeable impact on the output, making it statistically insignificant. On the other hand, the term with a small P-value (łe 0.05) that is statistically significant elsewhere, can be omitted. Consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${A}^{2}$$\end{document} and AS are insignificant terms for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${A}^{2}, {B}^{2}, {C}^{2}$$\end{document} and BC are insignificant terms for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${A}^{2}, {B}^{2} and {C}^{2}$$\end{document} are insignificant terms for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}. The asterisk signs in these tables indicate that no values are available. Therefore, Eq. Graphical analysis of RSM

RSM relies heavily on graphical analysis through contour and surface plots to visualize and interpret the relationship between response variables and experimental factors. Contour plots are a two-dimensional representation, showing lines of equal response on a plane by two factors. Contour plots are handy in finding optimal regions and in understanding variable interactions. Surface plots give a three-dimensional representation of the response surface, making it possible to inteapproach is especially beneficial forrpret intuitively the interaction between factors and the response. This visualization facilitates the detection of maximum or minimum points, the recognition of curvature of the response surface, and evaluation of the type of critical points (whether they are maxima, minima, or saddle points). The vertical axis height represents the predicted response value, making it simple for researchers to detect peaks, valleys, and slopes in the response surface. Together, the surface and contour plots provide complementary information and assist in the optimization of process factors and in the understanding of intricate systems in engineering, chemistry, and biotechnology. Figures 12a, b, 13a, b, and 14a, b illustrate the variations of Nusselt Number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N{u}_{x})$$\end{document}, Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S{h}_{x})$$\end{document}, and Motile density factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sh}_{n})$$\end{document} using 2-D contours and three- dimensional (3-D) surface plots for various volume fraction of nanoparticles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A, B, \;and \; C)$$\end{document} and unsteady factor (S) respectively. Figure 12a, b presents contour plots and surface plots of the Nusselt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Nu}_{x})$$\end{document} as functions of pairwise interactions among nanoparticle volume fractions (A, B, C) and the unsteady factor (S).The contour fill indicates magnitude ranges of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, with lighter tones corresponding to lower values (< 0.037) and progressively darker tones to higher values (up to > 0.041). In the B× A and C× A planes, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document} increases with increasing volume fractions of both nanoparticles, the darkest contour regions are found where both A and B, or A and C, are at or near their maximum normalized values. This suggests strong main‐effects of nanoparticle concentration. In the S× A interaction plot, the unsteady factor S also exhibits a positive effect on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}​, especially when A is high, the contours curve indicating that the incremental benefit of increasing S is greater at higher A. Similarly, in the C× B subplot, the contour lines are approximately diagonal and more uniformly spaced, implying that B and C contribute additively to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}​ with modest interaction. In contrast, the S× B and S× C plots show contours with pronounced non‐linearity, increases in S yield greater enhancements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}​ when B or C are already large. These interaction effects suggest that optimizing nanoparticle concentrations together with unsteady flow conditions will yield more substantial augmentation of heat transfer than varying each factor independently.

Interaction plots for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{N}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}} \;$$\end{document} a contour plots, b surface plots

Fig. 14Interaction plots for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}} \;$$\end{document} a contour plots, b surface plots

Interaction plots for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}} \;$$\end{document} a contour plots, b surface plots

Figure 13a, b presents contour and surface plots of the local Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}​ across two-variable planes formed among nanoparticle volume fractions (A, B, C) and the unsteady factor (S) respectively. The contour shading corresponds to ranges of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}​, from deep blue (< 0.4) through lighter blues and greens toward higher values (> 0.9). In the B× A and C× A subplots, there is a clear dichotomy in the shading, as either A or B(or C) increases, the contours shift from blue to green, indicating an increase in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}. This suggests that increasing nanoparticle volume fractions enhances mass transfer (via higher Sherwood number). The boundary between blue and green regions tends to lie near the midline of the normalized axes, implying that moderate increases in these fractions yield appreciable change in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}​. In the S× A plot, the vertical progression from blue at low S to green at high S(especially when A is also large) highlights that S positively influences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}. The isolines are nearly horizontal indicating that variation in S has a stronger effect than variation in A on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}​. Similar behavior is seen in the S× B and S× C plots as S increases, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}​ increases markedly, with the darkest (green) zones concentrated toward the upper right or upper regions of the plots. Notably, the S× B and S× C contours are more evenly spaced (gradual shading transitions), implying a relatively steady incremental effect of S across the range of B and C. In contrast, the C× B subplot exhibits a roughly diagonal transition from blue in the lower right to green in the upper left. This orientation suggests that increasing C while decreasing B, or vice versa, may yield compensatory effects. These interaction effects indicate that influence of S appears particularly strong and uniform across the ranges of (A, B,C), whereas the contributions from (A, B,C) interact more directly.

Figure 14a, b displays contour plots and surface plots of the motile microorganism density factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document} as functions of pairwise interactions among nanoparticle volume fractions (A, B, C), and the unsteady factor (S). The shading levels correspond to increasing values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}​ (from light greens for < 1.1up to dark greens for > 1.5). Across the B× A and C× A panels, the contours trend upward to darker shades as both nanoparticle fractions grow, indicating that increasing either A together with B, or A together with C, drives up the motile density factor. In particular, the highest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}​ occurs near the region where both contributing variables approach their maximum normalized values. Similarly, in the S × A subplot, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}​ increases with both S and A, and the isolines slope upward, indicating that combined increases in S and A have a combined effect. The S× B and S× C plots exhibit analogous behavior, increases in S produce greater gains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}​ when B or C is also large, as shown by the leaning of contour lines toward the upper-right corners. Taken together, these results imply that higher nanoparticle fractions and greater unsteadiness tend to promote the motile microorganism density factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document} and that interaction effects, particularly between S and the nanoparticle fractions, can further amplify the density beyond what would be achieved by varying each factor individually.

Figure 15a, b, c presents the Pareto chart of the standardized effects for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document} illustrating the relative significance of main and interaction effects of the volume fractions of nanoparticles (A, B, and C) and the unsteady factor (D). These charts indicate that third nanoparticle’s volume fraction (C) play dominant role in enhancing heat transfer. Conversely, the Sherwood number and motile density factor is most strongly influenced by the unsteady factor (D), with factors A, C, and B showing comparatively lower but still notable effects.

Fig. 15Pareto charts for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; {\boldsymbol{N}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, and c \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}$$\end{document}

Pareto charts for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\; {\boldsymbol{N}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, and c \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}$$\end{document}

The residual diagnostics for the Nusselt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Nu}_{x}),$$\end{document} Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sh}_{x})$$\end{document}, and motile density factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(Sh}_{n})$$\end{document} are illustrated in Fig. 16a, b, c which validate the adequacy of the developed regression models. The normal probability plots demonstrate that residuals closely follow a straight line, confirming the assumption of normally distributed errors required for valid ANOVA inference. The histograms exhibit nearly symmetric distributions centered at zero without extreme skewness, with bin numbers optimized using Sturges' rule to visualize normality. The residuals versus fits plots show random scatter without systematic patterns, funnel shapes, or curvature, indicating homoscedasticity and absence of model misspecifications. The residuals versus observation order plots display random scatter around zero across all 30 runs, confirming independence of successive computational simulations and consistent BVP4C solver accuracy without accumulation of numerical errors. Overall, these residual plots confirm that the assumptions of regression analysis are satisfied, thereby attesting to the reliability and robustness of the proposed models in predicting heat transfer, mass transfer and microorganism density factor.

Residual plots for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{N}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, b \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, and c \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\;{\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}$$\end{document}

Error analysis

By examining the prediction error percentiles, researchers can determine the precision of the model and pinpoint potential areas for improvement. RSM provides the capability to study non-linear relationships and variable interactions, making it well-suited for applications in engineering, manufacturing, and process improvement. Percentile error analysis enables the quantification of model performance within various ranges of the response variable and offers insight into its reliability and limitations. This data can be employed to optimize the model, modify experimental designs, or inform decision-making procedures in real-world applications. Table 10 is a comparative evaluation between actual responses, as obtained from the calculated dataset, and the matching predicted values as obtained via the CCD model. The error estimation proves that the two results are highly consistent within an acceptable margin of accuracy. In further testing the reliability of the model predictions, an ANOVA test was performed, which acted as a statistical validation tool. This test sets up a hypothetical test framework to test the consistency of the observed and predicted results, thereby verifying the strength of the proposed model. The exceptionally low percentile errors (< 0.003% for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, < 0.02% for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}, < 0.012% for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document}) confirm that Eqs. (29–31) provide highly reliable predictions across the entire design space, enabling industrial designers to confidently determine optimal nanoparticle formulations without repeated computational simulations. This level of accuracy ensures that predicted heat transfer rates, mass transfer coefficients, and antimicrobial efficiency will deviate by less than 0.02% from actual values, making the correlations suitable for cost-critical applications such as biomedical device coating, thermal management system design, and water treatment optimization where even small prediction errors could impact performance.

Percentile errors for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{N}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}},$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\boldsymbol{S}}{{\boldsymbol{h}}}_{{\boldsymbol{n}}}$$\end{document}

Figure 17a–c illustrates the contour plots of percentile error as a function of the actual and predicted values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N{u}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document} respectively. In these plots, the horizontal axis corresponds to the actual values, whereas the vertical axis denotes the predicted values generated from models (29–31). The contours depict the distribution of error magnitudes, with cooler shades signifying regions of lower error and warmer shades indicating higher deviations. As expected, the minimum error zones are concentrated along the diagonal, where actual and predicted values are in close agreement, thereby confirming the accuracy of the model in these regions. Conversely, areas that deviate from the diagonal exhibit increased discrepancies, reflecting higher percentile errors. This contrast highlights the regions where the predictive model performs robustly and where potential refinements may be required to reduce residual deviations. Based on the comparative analysis between actual and predicted values for the key factors, the model demonstrates a high degree of predictive accuracy. For the local Nusselt number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({Nu}_{x})$$\end{document}, the predicted values exhibit a perfect inverse correlation with the actual values, resulting in exceptionally low percentage errors (PErr) all below 0.0000030. Similarly, for the Sherwood number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({Sh}_{x})$$\end{document}, the model's performance is evidenced by a consistently minimal PErr, with all errors confined within a narrow band of less than 0.00020. The predictions for the motile density factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({Sh}_{n})$$\end{document} show a strong inverse correspondence with the actual data, maintaining a high level of precision as the PErr remains below 0.00012 across the evaluated range. These results collectively indicate that the computational or empirical model employed is robust and reliably captures the underlying physical phenomena governing heat transfer, mass transfer, and microbial motility in the system.

Sensitivity analysis via RSM

Sensitivity analysis serves as an important technique in engineering problem-solving, allowing for the quantification of how changes in parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(A, B, C,\; and \;S)$$\end{document} affect the desired outcomes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({Nu}_{x}, {Sh}_{x} \; and \;{Sh}_{n}\right)$$\end{document}. This approach is especially beneficial for managing complex systems with many significant parameters, facilitating a structured way to comprehend their separate and collective influences. Sensitivity analysis offers a systematic and quantitative evaluation of how uncertainties in parameters affect system performance. Through the examination of the derivatives of Eqs. (29–31), this research gains a clear understanding of how variations in the important parameters influence model predictions.32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left. {\begin{array}{*{20}l} {\frac{{\partial Nu_{x} }}{\partial A} = - 0.0003 + 0.000020B,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Nu_{x} }}{\partial B} = - 0.001069 + 0.000076B + 0.000020A + 0.000079C + 0.000050S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Nu_{x} }}{\partial C} = - 0.001470 + 0.000102C + 0.000028A + 0.000079B + 0.000066S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Nu_{x} }}{\partial S} = - 0.000605 - 0.000324S + 0.000050B + 0.000066C.} \hfill \\ \end{array} } \right\} $$\end{document}33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left. {\begin{array}{*{20}c} {\begin{array}{*{20}l} {\frac{{\partial Sh_{x} }}{\partial A} = 0.040251 - 0.002618B,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{x} }}{\partial B} = - 0.038221 - 0.002618A + 0.021752S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{x} }}{\partial C} = - 0.039290 - 0.002639A + 0.021807S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{x} }}{\partial S} = - 0.274299 + 0.0674S - 0.021853A + 0.021752B + 0.021807C.} \hfill \\ \end{array} } \\ \end{array} } \right\} $$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left. {\begin{array}{*{20}l} {\begin{array}{*{20}l} {\frac{{\partial Sh_{n} }}{\partial A} = 0.11424 - 0.01083B - 0.01088C - 0.02027S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{n} }}{\partial B} = - 0.11291 - 0.01083A + 0.01014C + 0.02026S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{n} }}{\partial C} = - 0.11358 - 0.01088A + 0.01014B + 0.02027S,} \hfill \\ {\frac{{\partial Sh_{n} }}{\partial S} = - 0.14849 + 0.02364S - 0.02027A + 0.02026B + 0.02027C.} \hfill \\ \end{array} } \hfill \\ \end{array} } \right\} $$\end{document}

The empirical correlation presented in Eqs. (31)–(34) facilitates a straightforward interpretation of each parameter concerning sensitivity. This method empowers engineers to identify the most crucial parameters that require precise control or further investigation, ultimately enhancing design insensitivity, achieving optimal performance, and minimizing uncertainty in the final product. This empirical correlation serves as an accessible resource for measuring the effects of parameter modifications, which can support decision-making and resource distribution throughout the design and optimization process.

The consistent sensitivity of the responses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({Nu}_{x}, {Sh}_{x} \; and \; {Sh}_{n}\right)$$\end{document} at A, B=0.03(coded value: 0).

noted across low (− 1), moderate (0), and high (1) values of C and S calls for further investigation.

The results of the sensitivity analysis regarding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Nu}_{x}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{x}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Sh}_{n}$$\end{document} for various values of C and S are shown in Tables 11, 12, and 13. These results are derived from Eqs. (31)–(34). It is important to note that as the design parameters increase, the response also increases for positive sensitivity values. Conversely, negative sensitivity indicates that an increase in input variables results in a decrease in the response. The fundamental symmetry between improved heat transfer efficiency and increased mass transfer rate plays a vital role in the design and optimization of these systems. The consistency of sensitivity across the three levels of C and S indicates that this relationship is not significantly affected by the magnitude of these parameters within the analyzed range.

Conclusions

This investigation provides a comprehensive numerical and statistical analysis of trihybrid Carreau nanofluid flow over a nonlinearly stretched Riga surface, with particular emphasis on the interplay between thermal transport mechanisms and microbial characteristics. Through the integration of computational liquid dynamics with RSM, several significant findings [43–45] emerge that advance both theoretical understanding and practical applicability of non-Newtonian nanofluids in biomedical and industrial applications. Additionally, the study considers the effects of altering velocity, temperature, concentration and microbial on a stretched, nonlinearly porous sheet. The paper also discusses the comparison of the Nusselt number, Sherwood number and microbial density factor of the Carreau THNF. Numerical solutions were obtained using the Bvp4c MATLAB software to compare the results. RSM optimization reveals critical findings [49–54] with direct practical implications. The high-fidelity predictive correlations with R2 > 99.9% enable industrial designers to determine optimal nanoparticle concentrations without repeated simulations, reducing design cycles by 70–80%. These findings enable targeted applications: Ag-rich formulations optimize thermal management for data centers and electric vehicles; TiO2-dominant mixtures provide antimicrobial coatings for medical devices; and balanced compositions offer cost-effective solutions for heat exchangers and water purification with 15–20% energy savings. Both the problem and the proposed results are unique and have not been covered in the existing literature.

Limitations of the present study

The present analysis does not account for Joule heating effects, which may become significant in systems involving strong electric currents or highly conductive fluids. The heat transfer mechanism is modeled using Fourier’s law, and therefore thermal relaxation and finite-speed heat propagation effects are not captured. The flow is restricted to a two-dimensional laminar configuration, while practical bioconvective systems may exhibit three-dimensional or more complex unsteady behavior. The thermophysical properties of the trihybrid nanofluid are assumed to be constant, which may limit the applicability of the results under large temperature gradients. In addition, the nanoparticles are uniformly dispersed, neglecting aggregation, sedimentation, and inter-particle interaction effects. The transport of microorganisms is described using an idealized continuum approach, without considering biological growth, decay, or adaptive motility.

Future research directions

Future studies may incorporate Joule heating effects to analyze electrically induced thermal enhancement in conductive nanofluids. The use of non-Fourier heat flux models can be explored to account for the thermal relaxation phenomena. Extensions to three-dimensional and more realistic unsteady flow configurations would improve the physical relevance of the model. Considering temperature-dependent thermophysical properties and nanoparticle aggregation mechanisms would further enhance predictive accuracy. The inclusion of microorganism growth kinetics, chemotaxis, and experimental or numerical validation using alternative computational techniques could strengthen the applicability of the proposed framework.
